Supplement to “The weak-feature-impact phase transition of the NPMLE in monotone binary regression”
Dario Kieffer, Angelika Rohde, Dario Kieffer, Angelika Rohde

Abstract
Statistical literature provides pointwise limiting distributions of the nonparametric maximum likelihood estimator (NPMLE) in monotone binary regression for the two extremal cases: If the feature-label relation is strictly monotone and sufficiently smooth, it converges at a nonparametric rate with scaled Chernoff-type limiting distribution, and it converges at the parametric n \sqrt{n} -rate if the underlying relation is flat. In this article, we provide the complete picture of the distributional metamorphosis of the NPMLE, revealing a new limiting distribution which provides a significantly better distributional approximation for small samples in case of a weak feature-label relationship. It is shown to continuously interpolate between the two extremal cases. The innovative way to determine this distribution is to generate it as a limit of the NPMLE in the newly introduced weak-feature-impact triangular array for a particular parameter-sample-size constellation. Moreover, a phase transition is likewise observed for the suitably rescaled L 1 L^{1} -error in this weak-feature-impact scenario. As a by-product, its limiting distribution for flat regression functions is obtained, which was unknown before. The proof develops a completely new strategy, notably not based on the switch relation. A novel type of local minimax lower bounds accompanies these results.



keywords

Binary regression model
; convergence rate
; distributional metamorphosis
; limiting distribution
; nonparametric maximum likelihood estimation
; phase transition
; weak feature impact

??
Albert-Ludwigs-Universität Freiburg, ??, ??

1
Introduction

Statistical literature provides the pointwise limiting distributions of the nonparametric maximum likelihood estimator (NPMLE) in monotone binary regression for the two extremal cases: If the feature-label relation is strictly monotone and sufficiently smooth, it converges pointwise at a nonparametric rate with scaled Chernoff-type limiting distribution, while it converges at the parametric
\sqrt{n}
-rate if the underlying relation is flat. Figure
1
indicates that the flatter the slope of the strictly monotone regression function, the later the established Chernoff approximation kicks in (lightblue versus blue line compared to the Chernoff distribution function in black).

Figure 1:
Let
X\sim\mathcal{U}([-1,1])
,
\Phi(x)=\textrm{logit}^{-1}(\delta x)
the regression function,
\hat{\Phi}_{n}
the NPMLE for
\Phi
and
x_{0}=1/4
. Note that the regression function’s derivative scales as
\delta
. The black line shows the Chernoff distribution function. The distribution function of the random variable
(2n)^{1/3}(4\Phi(x_{0})(1-\Phi(x_{0}))\Phi^{\prime}(x_{0}))^{-1/3}(\hat{\Phi}_{n}(x_{0})-\Phi(x_{0}))
is simulated for
\delta=3
(lightblue) and
\delta=1/15
(blue) based on
2000
iterations of i.i.d. samples of size
n
.

The goal of this article is to explain this small-sample phenomenon on a rigorous mathematical basis and to develop more appropriate distributional approximations for small sample sizes, both pointwise and in
L^{1}
, as well as to specify in particular to which extent these depend on unknown oracle information. Based on our new insights on the distribution of the NPMLE, we shall discuss their statistical implications and open problems deduced from them. Note that the situation of a weakly increasing regression function corresponds to a weak feature-label relationship, which occurs frequently in applications. For example, privacy preserving requirements may diminish the isolated effect of an explanatory variable
X
on the response variable
Y
considerably.

1.1
State of the art

As the problem of estimating a monotone function arises naturally in many real world tasks and also builds the foundation
for multiple statistical models, it has been studied extensively over the last decades, with
Grenander (1957)

being the first to consider the NPMLE for monotone densities, lending it the name
Grenander estimator
. It was
shown first in
Prakasa Rao (1969)
that this estimator is
n^{1/3}
-consistent with respect to the pointwise distance
and asymptotically Chernoff-distributed if the density’s first derivative does not vanish. This was then proven again in

Groeneboom (1985)
by a different technique utilizing inverse expressions based on the
switch relation
,
which became the most important tool for deriving limits of the NPMLE under various
shape constraints. In that article, the
L^{1}
-limiting behavior was considered for the first time and a rigorous
proof of the
L^{1}
-limit appeared in
Groeneboom et al. (1999)
, showing that the expectation of the
L^{1}
-distance
converges with rate
n^{1/3}
to zero and that the stabilized
L^{1}
-distance itself fluctuates with rate
n^{1/6}
and is asymptotically normal. A generalization to the
L^{p}
-distance was given in
Kulikov and Lopuhaä (2005)
.
Similar results regarding the pointwise distance appeared in the context of isotonic regression and least
squares estimation (LSE) in
Brunk (1970)
and for current status data in
Groeneboom and Wellner (1992)
, utilizing
that NPMLE and LSE coincide here. A unified study of various estimators, including the monotone NPMLE, was
introduced in
Kim and Pollard (1990)
. The
L^{1}
-limit for isotonic regression with fixed design was derived in

Durot (2002)
and was later generalized to the
L^{p}
-distance in
Durot (2007)
and to the random design
setting in
Durot (2008)
.

Many more properties of the NPMLE under monotonicity constraints were derived, e.g. the pointwise limiting behavior for functions with vanishing derivative up to some order

\beta
in
Wright (1981)
, resulting in convergence rates
n^{\beta/(2\beta+1)}
, and for
locally flat densities in
Carolan and Dykstra (1999)
, yielding
\sqrt{n}
-consistency. Non-asymptotic
properties were discovered in
Birgé (1989)
, local minimax-optimality was derived in
Cator (2011)
and
Chatterjee et al. (2015)
for the local and global estimation problem, respectively and
Bellec (2018)
derived sharp oracle inequalities in Euclidean norm for the LSE of isotonic vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. The limiting behavior under the uniform distance was derived in

Durot et al. (2012)
and the misspecified case was studied in
Patilea (2001)
and
Jankowski (2014)
. More information can be found in the overview articles
Groeneboom and Jongbloed (2018)
,

Durot and Lopuhaä (2018)
and
Groeneboom and Jongbloed (2014)
.

In
Westling and Carone (2020)
, a unified approach to study generalized Grenander
estimators was introduced.
Mallick et al. (2023)
generated new pointwise limiting distributions in
the nonparametric regime for
n
-dependent monotone functions with possibly locally changing shape, not reaching the parametric regime, however. Based on this, asymptotic confidence intervals that are uniformly valid over a large class of distributions are constructed. Using a new localization technique in
isotonic regression and an anti-concentration inequality for the supremum of a Brownian motion with a Lipschitz drift,

Han and Kato (2022)
derived Berry-Esseen bounds for Chernoff-type limiting distributions.

Cattaneo et al. (2024)
proposed a bootstrap adapting to the unknown order of the first non-zero derivative.

1.2
The weak-feature-impact scenario

As it becomes apparent from Section
1.1
, the literature on the NPMLE does not adequately explain the small-sample effect observed in Figure
1
. In this section, we introduce the so-called weak-feature-impact scenario along which we will rigorously discover and characterize the full distributional spectrum of the NPMLE. In order to motivate our setting, it is advisable to recall classical logistic regression, where the feature-label relation is given by
\mathbb{P}(Y=1|X=x)=\textrm{logit}^{-1}(c+\delta^{\top}x)
. A natural generalization is to replace the logistic function by an arbitrary isotonic
[0,1]
-valued function
\Phi_{0}
, i.e.
\mathbb{P}(Y=1|X=x)=\Phi_{0}(\delta^{\top}x)
, where likewise the magnitude of the components of
\delta
control the predictive power of the corresponding feature components in a global sense. Clearly, the extremal case of no impact is present if
\delta=0
. The model reduces to monotone binary regression in the univariate case.
As the distribution of the NPMLE in monotone binary regression is essentially accessible subject to asymptotics, the magnitude of the global feature impact has to scale with the sample size
n
to preserve the small-sample effect observed in Figure
1
in an asymptotic sense (see Figure
2
).

Figure 2:
In the same setting as in Figure
1
with the additional grey line visualizing the distribution function of the limiting distribution for flat functions and again with regression function
\Phi(x)=\textrm{logit}^{-1}(\delta x)
, the small-sample effect on the distribution of the NPMLE freezes when
n\delta^{2}=\text{const.}

Let
(\Omega,\mathcal{A},\mathbb{P})
denote a probability space and consider the triangular array
(X_{1},Y_{1}^{n}),\dots,(X_{n},Y_{n}^{n})
of respective i.i.d. copies of a random vector

(X,Y^{n})\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R}\times\{0,1\}
, related via

\mathbb{P}\big(Y^{n}=1|X\big)=\Phi_{0}(\delta_{n}X)=\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}\Phi_{n}(X)

(1.1)

for some isotonic function
\Phi_{0}
and a stretching sequence
(\delta_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
with
\delta_{n}\searrow 0
. We call the
sequence
(\delta_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
the
level of feature impact
, as it represents — in the style of the logistic regression model — the rate at which the signal strength decreases and thus, controls the predictive power of the feature. Subsequently, this asymptotic scheme is referred to as
weak-feature-impact scenario
. As Figure
2
illustrates, it allows to strikingly describe the small-sample effect in an asymptotic setting. Indeed, an appropriate distributional approximation for the small-sample phenomenon observed in Figure
1
appears to be an intermediate state in the metamorphosis from the strictly isotonic to the flat case. This requires us to fully understand this metamorphosis in mathematical terms. If
\Phi_{0}
is continuously differentiable, the derivative of (
1.1
) with respect to the feature variable satisfies

\Phi_{n}^{\prime}(x)=\delta_{n}\Phi_{0}^{\prime}(\delta_{n}x)=\delta_{n}\big(\Phi_{0}^{\prime}(0)+o(1)\big)\longrightarrow 0\quad\text{as }n\longrightarrow\infty.

Thus, if
\Phi_{0}^{\prime}>0
, the level of feature impact characterizes the speed at which the derivative of the function

x\mapsto\mathbb{P}(Y^{n}=1|X=x)
approaches zero, uniformly on compacts. Note that a weak feature-label relation is a global property and hence, cannot be modeled locally solely.

1.3
Overview of the results

In this article, we study the pointwise error
\hat{\Phi}_{n}(x)-\Phi_{n}(x)
of the NPMLE (Section
3
) as well as its appropriately normalized
L^{1}
-error (Section
4
) in the weak-feature impact triangular array, which will be shown to bring different limiting distributions of the NPMLE into being. Assuming for the ease of representation that
\Phi_{0}^{\prime}>0
, our first finding is that pointwise and in
L^{1}
, the full picture is governed by the quantity
n\delta_{n}^{2}
: We identify three regimes, the slow regime
n\delta_{n}^{2}\longrightarrow\infty
, the fast regime
n\delta_{n}^{2}\longrightarrow 0
and the intermediate regime
n\delta_{n}^{2}\longrightarrow c\in(0,\infty)
together with the rate of consistency

\Big(\frac{n}{\delta_{n}}\Big)^{1/3}\wedge\sqrt{n}

both pointwise and in
L^{1}
, which is shown to coincide with a new type of minimax lower bounds (Theorem
3.1
and Theorem
4.1
). In the slow regime, the limiting distribution coincides with the limit for fixed strictly isotonic functions (
\delta_{n}=\text{const.}
), while it coincides with the limit for flat functions (
\delta_{n}=0)
in the fast regime.

Our main contributions for the pointwise asymptotics (Section
3
) are as follows:

\bullet

We find a new distribution at the phase transition in the intermediate regime, which adequately fits the NPMLE for small sample sizes in case of a weak feature-label relationship (orange line in Figure
3
). This distribution is different from both, the Chernoff distribution and the distribution known for flat regression functions. The innovative way to determine this distribution is to generate it as a limit of the NPMLE in the newly introduced weak-feature-impact triangular array for a particular parameter-sample-size constellation.

\bullet

Although valuable local adaptivity properties of the NPMLE have been documented in the literature, the question about the corresponding picture in terms of approximating distributions has been totally open. Theorem
3.2
answers this question by providing the complete picture of the distributional metamorphosis, while Theorem
3.3
confirms the continuity of the transformation between the two extremal cases (see Figure
3
).

\bullet

These results are accompanied by a new type of local minimax lower bounds.

Figure 3:
Let
X\sim\mathcal{U}([-1,1])
,
\Phi(x)=\textrm{logit}^{-1}(\delta x)
and
x_{0}=0
. The black line shows the Chernoff distribution function, while the grey and the orange line respectively show a simulated distribution function of the limit for flat functions and the newly uncovered limit. The blue line shows a simulated distribution function of the appropriately scaled NPMLE
\{(n/\Phi^{\prime}(x_{0}))^{1/3}\wedge\sqrt{n}\}(\hat{\Phi}_{n}(x_{0})-\Phi(x_{0}))
. Exclusively if
n\delta^{2}
is large, the approximation with the scaled Chernoff distribution is appropriate. When
n\delta^{2}
is small, the limiting distribution for flat functions is a better approximation, even in the non-flat case. In between, however, both fail to be a good approximation. All simulations are calculated based on
2000
iterations of i.i.d. samples of size
n=30
.

Our main contributions for the
L^{1}
-asymptotics (Section
4
) are as follows:

\bullet

In the fast regime (Theorem
4.3
(ii)), the established limiting distribution is new and has not even been discovered in classical asymptotics for flat functions.
Our proof in here develops a completely new strategy, notably not based on the switch relation.

\bullet

The proof for the slow regime (Theorem
4.3
(i)) uncovers a previously unknown interplay of the convergence rate
(n/\delta_{n})^{1/3}
of the NPMLE and the newly derived convergence rate
(n\delta_{n}^{2})^{1/3}
of the inverse process, which cannot be read off from classical asymptotics where these two rates coincide.

\bullet

Again, the results are accompanied by a new type of minimax lower bounds in
L^{1}
(Theorem
4.1
) with the proof based on a non-standard choice of hypotheses in Assouad’s lemma.

Moreover, our results across Section
3
and Section
4
reveal that the limits in the slow regime coincide with the respective limits known from classical asymptotics of strictly increasing regression functions, while the scalings stand out from the classical cases and are different for local and global asymptotics. Whereas the rate of convergence is getting faster in the pointwise case, the rate of convergence
(n\delta_{n}^{2})^{1/6}
of the stabilized
L^{1}
-distance towards the limiting distribution slows down and collapses at the phase transition.

The remaining part of the article is organized as follows. In Section
2
, we introduce notation and present some basics of the NPMLE. In particular, a uniform version of Hellinger consistency is stated and
uniform convergence on compacts in the weak-feature-impact scenario is deduced.
In Sections
3
and
4
, we state convergence
rates and limiting distributions for the pointwise and the
L^{1}
-distance, respectively, as outlined in
Section
1.3
, together with matching minimax lower bounds that are uniform over a family of appropriate subclasses of monotone functions. In Section
5
, we discuss further statistical implications of our results, as well as some open problems.
Remaining proofs and auxiliary results are deferred to the supplement.

2
Notation and preliminaries on the NPMLE

Let
P_{\Phi}
denote the joint distribution of
(X,Y)
with
\mathbb{P}(Y=1|X)=\Phi(X)
and feature-marginal
P_{X}
, and let
P_{\Phi}^{\otimes n}
denote the
n
-fold product measure with expectation operator
\mathbb{E}^{\otimes n}_{\Phi}
. For the remainder of the article, we write
F_{X}
for the distribution function of
P_{X}
and

\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}
for its support. It is assumed that
P_{X}
is Lebesgue-continuous and we write
p_{X}
for the continuous version of the Lebesgue density on
\mathcal{X}
if it
exists. For
F_{n}
denoting the empirical distribution function of
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
, we define

F_{n}^{-1}\colon[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
,
F_{n}^{-1}(a)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\inf\{x\in\mathbb{R}\mid F_{n}(x)\geq a\}

as usual. Moreover, we write

\mathcal{F}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\{\Phi\colon\mathbb{R}\to[0,1]\mid\Phi\text{ monotonically increasing}\}

for the set of monotonically increasing functions from
\mathbb{R}
into the unit interval. For
\Phi\in\mathcal{F}
,

p_{\Phi}\colon\mathbb{R}\times\{0,1\}\to[0,1],\quad p_{\Phi}(x,y)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\Phi(x)^{y}(1-\Phi(x))^{1-y}

is the conditional probability mass function of
Y
given
X
if
(X,Y)\sim P_{\Phi}
. In the product experiment, the NPMLE for feature-label realizations

(x_{1},y_{1}),\dots,(x_{n},y_{n})
is defined as

\hat{\Phi}_{n}\in\operatorname*{Argmax}_{\Phi\in\mathcal{F}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}p_{\Phi}(x_{i},y_{i})=\operatorname*{Argmax}_{\Phi\in\mathcal{F}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log p_{\Phi}(x_{i},y_{i}).

Note that in the weak-feature-impact scenario, as introduced in Section
1.2
, the
n
observations are realized according to
P_{\Phi_{n}}^{\otimes n}
and the
resulting NPMLE is an estimator for
\Phi_{n}
. Its existence and uniqueness at the sample points (in case the
x_{i}
are pairwise different) can be proven as in Part II Prop. 1.1 & Prop. 1.2 of

Groeneboom and Wellner (1992)
.
As usual in the literature, we agree on
\hat{\Phi}_{n}
being right-continuous and piecewise constant with jumping points
being a subset of the sample points, i.e. for the order statistic
x_{(1)},\dots,x_{(n)}
of
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
,

\displaystyle\hat{\Phi}_{n}|_{(-\infty,x_{(1)})}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=0,\qquad\hat{\Phi}_{n}|_{[x_{(i)},x_{(i+1)})}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\hat{\Phi}_{n}(x_{(i)}),\qquad\hat{\Phi}_{n}|_{[x_{(n)},\infty)}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\hat{\Phi}_{n}(x_{(n)})

(2.1)

for
i=1,\dots,n-1
. Although there is no closed-form expression for
\hat{\Phi}_{n}
, it is possible to characterize the
NPMLE under monotonicity constraints as follows: Let
y_{(1)},\dots,y_{(n)}
be the corresponding ordering of the labels
according to
x_{(1)},\dots,x_{(n)}
(i.e. if
x_{(i)}=x_{j}
for some
1\leq j\leq n
, then
y_{(i)}=y_{j}
),
let

\mathcal{Y}_{n}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\bigg\{\bigg(\frac{i}{n},\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{i}y_{(j)}\bigg)\bigg|\ \,i\in\{1,\dots,n\}\bigg\}\cup\big\{(0,0)\big\}

and let
G_{n}\colon[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
denote the greatest convex minorant of
\mathcal{Y}_{n}
. Then,
\hat{\Phi}_{n}(x_{(i)})
is
given by the left-hand derivative of
G_{n}
in the point
i/n
, i.e.

\hat{\Phi}_{n}(x_{(i)})=\sup_{s<\frac{i}{n}}\inf_{t\geq\frac{i}{n}}\frac{G_{n}(t)-G_{n}(s)}{t-s}.

(2.2)

In particular,
\hat{\Phi}_{n}
coincides with the local average of the labels between two jumping points.

Generally, we write
g^{*}
for the greatest convex minorant of a continuous function
g\colon I\to\mathbb{R}
for some
interval
I\subset\mathbb{R}
and denote its left-hand derivative by
g^{*,\ell}
, which is given as in
(
2.2
),
but with
G_{n}
replaced by
g
. We refer to Chapter 3.3 of
Groeneboom and Jongbloed (2014)
for more details on this.
From Lemma 3.2 of
Groeneboom and Jongbloed (2014)
, we obtain the
switch relation
, giving an
expression for the generalized inverse of
g^{*,\ell}
. Let

\operatorname*{argmin^{+}}
denote the supremum of all minimizers.

Lemma 2.1

(Switch relation)
.

For every
x
in the interior of
I
and any
a\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

g^{*,\ell}(x)>a\quad\iff\quad\operatorname*{argmin^{+}}_{u\in I}\{g(u)-au\}<x.

Similarly, two different characterizations of the generalized inverse of
\hat{\Phi}_{n}
have been established in the
literature, with
Groeneboom (1985)
being the first to introduce such an inverse process. Following
Section 4.1 in
Durot (2008)
, let
\Upsilon_{n}\colon[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
denote the polygonal chain with

(i/n,\Upsilon_{n}(i/n))\in\mathcal{Y}_{n}
for
i=1,\dots,n
and let
g_{n}\colon[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
denote the left-hand derivative
of
G_{n}
. Then, by definition, we have
\hat{\Phi}_{n}(X_{(i)})=g_{n}(i/n)=g_{n}\circ F_{n}(X_{(i)})
for
i=1,\dots,n
. Define

\displaystyle U_{n}\colon[0,1]\to\mathbb{R},\quad U_{n}(a)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\operatorname*{argmin^{+}}_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\bigg\{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{i}^{n}\mathds{1}_{\{X_{i}\leq x\}}-aF_{n}(x)\bigg\},

\displaystyle\tilde{U}_{n}\colon[0,1]\to\mathbb{R},\quad\tilde{U}_{n}(a)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\operatorname*{argmin^{+}}_{t\in[0,1]}\{\Upsilon_{n}(t)-at\}

(2.3)

and note that
F_{n}^{-1}\circ\tilde{U}_{n}(a)=U_{n}(a)
, as
\tilde{U}_{n}
maps into the set

\{i/n\mid i=0,\dots,n\}
and the process inside the
\operatorname*{argmin^{+}}_{x\in\mathcal{X}}
in the definition of
U_{n}
changes its values only at the observation points. For completeness, the proof of the subsequent formulation of the switch relation is given in Section
A.1
.

Lemma 2.2
.

For every
x\in\mathcal{X}
and any
a\in[0,1]
, we have

\hat{\Phi}_{n}(x)>a\quad\iff\quad U_{n}(a)<F_{n}^{-1}(F_{n}(x))\quad\iff\quad F_{n}^{-1}\circ\tilde{U}_{n}(a)<F_{n}^{-1}(F_{n}(x))\qquad P_{\Phi}^{\otimes n}-\text{a.s.}

One particularly important property of the NPMLE, which paves the way for our later study, is Hellinger-consistency
uniformly in
\Phi
.
Let
h
denote the Hellinger metric, i.e.

h^{2}(P_{\Phi},P_{\Psi})=\frac{1}{2}\hskip-1.5649pt\int_{\mathbb{R}}\hskip-3.41432pt\big(\sqrt{1-\Phi(x)}-\sqrt{1-\Psi(x)}\big)^{2}+\big(\sqrt{\Phi(x)}-\sqrt{\Psi(x)}\big)^{2}dP_{X}(x)=\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}d^{2}(\Phi,\Psi)

(2.4)

for any
\Phi,\Psi\in\mathcal{F}
, inducing the semi-metric
d
on
\mathcal{F}
.

Proposition 2.3

(Uniform Hellinger consistency)
.

For any
\varepsilon>0
, the NPMLE satisfies

\sup_{\Phi\in\mathcal{F}}P_{\Phi}^{\otimes n}\big(d(\hat{\Phi}_{n},\Phi)>\varepsilon\big)\longrightarrow 0\quad\text{as }n\longrightarrow\infty.

The result might be well-known, yet we did not find it stated in the uniform version as formulated here.
Hence, we give a proof in Section
A.2
. Because Hellinger distance dominates total variation, Proposition
2.3
reveals for any
\varepsilon>0
likewise

\sup_{\Phi\in\mathcal{F}}P_{\Phi}^{\otimes n}\big(\|\hat{\Phi}_{n}-\Phi\|_{L^{1}(P_{X})}>\varepsilon\big)\longrightarrow 0\quad\text{as }n\longrightarrow\infty.

(2.5)

As a consequence,
d(\hat{\Phi}_{n},\Phi_{n})\longrightarrow_{\mathbb{P}}0
and
\|\hat{\Phi}_{n}-\Phi_{n}\|_{L^{1}(P_{X})}\longrightarrow_{\mathbb{P}}0
in the weak-feature-impact scenario, irrespective of the level of feature impact.

Corollary 2.4
.

Let
\Phi_{0}
be continuous in a neighborhood of zero. Then, for any compact interval
I
in the interior of
\mathcal{X}
,

\sup_{x\in I}|\hat{\Phi}_{n}(x)-\Phi_{n}(x)|\longrightarrow_{\mathbb{P}}0\quad\text{as }n\longrightarrow\infty

in the weak-feature-impact scenario.

The proof is given in Section
A.3
, where we
design a tricky two-stage
subsequence argument to deduce pointwise convergence from (
2.5
) in the weak-feature-impact scenario at any interior point of
\mathcal{X}
. The result then follows from the fact that pointwise convergent
[0,1]
-valued isotonic functions with
continuous limit also converge uniformly on compacts.

Throughout from now on,
P_{X}
is assumed to be compactly supported on

\mathcal{X}=[-T,T]
for some
T>0
with continuous, strictly positive Lebesgue density
p_{X}
on
\mathcal{X}
.

3
The local case

3.1
Pointwise local minimax lower bounds

Recall that a crucial consequence of the weak-feature-impact scenario is that the level of feature impact controls the gradient of the feature-label relation uniformly on compacts, both from above and from below. This raises the question which rate of convergence is attainable in principle by any estimator over restricted classes reflecting this constraint.
For any function
f\in\mathcal{F}
, let

\|f\|_{\mathcal{X},L}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y\in\mathcal{X}:\\
x<y\end{subarray}}\frac{f(y)-f(x)}{y-x}\ \ \text{and}\ \ \omega_{\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}}^{\mathcal{X}}(f)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\sup\big\{f(y)-f(x)\mid x,y\in\mathcal{X},0<y-x\leq\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}\big\}

(3.1)

denote Lipschitz semi-norm and modulus of continuity of its restriction to
\mathcal{X}
, respectively. For any
\delta\in[0,1]
, let

\mathcal{F}_{\delta}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\Big\{\Phi\in\mathcal{F}\ \Big|\ \|\Phi\|_{\mathcal{X},L}\leq\delta\text{ and }\inf_{\nu}\omega_{\nu}^{\mathcal{X}}(\Phi)/\nu\geq\delta/2\Big\}

(3.2)

and note that
\mathcal{F}_{\delta}
contains only functions of
\mathcal{F}
with steepness of order
\delta
. Due to the condition on the modulus of continuity, the steepness of these functions is not only controlled from above, but also from below.
We remark that for continuously differentiable
\Phi_{0}
with
\Phi_{0}^{\prime}(0)>0
,
\Phi_{n}=\Phi_{0}(\delta_{n}\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}})\in\mathcal{F}_{\kappa\delta_{n}}
for any
\kappa\in(\Phi_{0}^{\prime}(0),2\Phi_{0}^{\prime}(0))
and
n>n_{0}(\kappa)
sufficiently large.

Theorem 3.1

(Pointwise lower bound)
.

For any
x_{0}
contained in the interior of
\mathcal{X}
, there exists a positive constant

C>0
, such that

\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\inf_{\delta\in[0,\frac{1}{4T}]}\inf_{T_{n}^{\delta}(x_{0})}\sup_{\Phi\in\mathcal{F}_{\delta}}P_{\Phi}^{\otimes n}\Big(\Big(\sqrt{n}\wedge\Big(\frac{n}{\delta}\Big)^{1/3}\Big)\big|T_{n}^{\delta}(x_{0})-\Phi(x_{0})\big|\geq C\Big)>0,

where the infimum is running over all estimators
T_{n}^{\delta}(x_{0})=T_{n}^{\delta}\big(x_{0},(x_{1},y_{1}),\dots,(x_{n},y_{n})\big)
.

While this lower bound remains true when enlarging
\mathcal{F}_{\delta}
by dropping the lower bound constraint on the modulus of continuity, this sharper formulation suitably complements the subsequent convergence rate in the weak-feature-impact scenario, as the latter is not stated in a uniform sense.
Note that the range of
\delta
necessarily has to be bounded from above as
\sharp\mathcal{F}_{\delta}<2
for
\delta\geq 1/T
.
The proof is given in Section
B.1
. The lower bound exhibits an elbow at
\delta=\delta_{n}\sim n^{-1/2}
separating two regimes — the slow regime
(n/\delta)^{-1/3}
in case
\delta\gg n^{-1/2}
and the fast regime
n^{-1/2}
for
\delta\ll n^{-1/2}
with the intermediate regime at
\delta\asymp n^{-1/2}
. Note that by smoothing out the kinks in the respective lower bound hypotheses, the result continues to hold when restricted to continuously differentiable functions.

3.2
The metamorphosis of the pointwise limiting distribution

In view of the valuable pointwise adaptivity properties of the NPMLE in
Cator (2011)
, it does not come as a surprise that the above stated faster rate (as compared to the
n^{-1/3}
-rate in the unrestricted case) is actually adaptively attained by the NPMLE in the weak-feature-impact scenario. In this section, we complement the rates with the complete picture in terms of limiting distributions. To state the result, let
\sigma_{\Phi_{0}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\sqrt{\Phi_{0}(0)(1-\Phi_{0}(0))}
, let
(Z(s))_{s\in\mathbb{R}}
denote a
standard two-sided Brownian motion and let for
\beta\in\mathbb{N}
and any
c\geq 0
,

\displaystyle f_{\beta}\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R},\quad f_{\beta}(s)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\sigma_{\Phi_{0}}Z(s)+\frac{\Phi_{0}^{(\beta)}(0)}{p_{X}(x_{0})^{\beta}(\beta+1)!}s^{\beta+1}

\displaystyle g_{\beta,c}\colon[0,1]\to\mathbb{R},\quad g_{\beta,c}(s)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\sigma_{\Phi_{0}}Z(s)+\sqrt{c}\,\Phi_{0}^{(\beta)}(0)\mathbb{E}\big[(X-x_{0})^{\beta}\mathds{1}_{\{X\leq F_{X}^{-1}(s)\}}\big].

Theorem 3.2
.

For
\beta\in\mathbb{N}
, let
x_{0}
be an interior point of
\mathcal{X}
and assume
\Phi_{0}
to be
\beta
-times continuously
differentiable in a neighborhood of zero with the
\beta
th derivative being the first non-vanishing derivative in zero.

(i)

(Slow regime) If
n\delta_{n}^{2\beta}\longrightarrow\infty
, then

\Big(\frac{n}{\delta_{n}}\Big)^{\beta/(2\beta+1)}\big(\hat{\Phi}_{n}(x_{0})-\Phi_{n}(x_{0})\big)\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{L}}f_{\beta}^{*,\ell}(0)\quad\text{as }n\longrightarrow\infty.

(ii)

(Intermediate regime)
If
n\delta_{n}^{2\beta}\longrightarrow c\in(0,\infty)
and
F_{X}^{-1}
is Hölder-continuous to the exponent
\alpha>1/2
, then

\sqrt{n}\big(\hat{\Phi}_{n}(x_{0})-\Phi_{n}(x_{0})\big)\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{L}}g_{\beta,c}^{*,\ell}(F_{X}(x_{0}))\quad\text{as }n\longrightarrow\infty.

(iii)

(Fast regime) If
n\delta_{n}^{2\beta}\longrightarrow 0
, then

\sqrt{n}\big(\hat{\Phi}_{n}(x_{0})-\Phi_{n}(x_{0})\big)\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{L}}g_{\beta,0}^{*,\ell}(F_{X}(x_{0}))\quad\text{as }n\longrightarrow\infty.

The proof of the theorem is given in Section
B.2
.
The result identifies three regimes, each of them with a different limiting distribution. Note that if
\Phi_{0}
is continuously differentiable with
\Phi_{0}^{\prime}(0)>0
, the convergence rate of the NPMLE equals, in correspondence to the minimax lower bound,

\sqrt{n}\wedge\Big(\frac{n}{\delta_{n}}\Big)^{1/3}.

Corresponding to the elbow in this rate, the limiting distribution exhibits a phase transition. The elbow is shifted to

\delta_{n}=n^{-1/(2\beta)}
if the
\beta
th derivative of
\Phi_{0}
for some
\beta>1
is the
first non-vanishing derivative at zero. The distribution of
f_{\beta}^{*,\ell}(0)
in (i) (slow regime) appeared first in
Wright (1981)
and is the well-known Chernoff-type limit (in the terminology of
Han and Kato (2022)
) of the NPMLE in
classical asymptotics under these general conditions on the derivative of the function to estimate, in consonance with
Theorem 2.2 in
Mallick et al. (2023)
.
We remark that by the switch relation (Lemma
2.1
) and Lemma
G.4
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(f_{1}^{*,\ell}(0)\leq v\big)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\bigg(\operatorname*{argmin}_{s\in\mathbb{R}}\Big\{\sigma_{\Phi_{0}}\sqrt{p_{X}(x_{0})}Z(s)+\frac{\Phi_{0}^{\prime}(0)p_{X}(x_{0})}{2}s^{2}-vp_{X}(x_{0})s\Big\}\geq 0\bigg)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\bigg(\bigg(\frac{4\sigma_{\Phi_{0}}^{2}\Phi_{0}^{\prime}(0)}{p_{X}(x_{0})}\bigg)^{1/3}\operatorname*{argmin}_{s\in\mathbb{R}}\big\{Z(s)+s^{2}\big\}\leq v\bigg)

for any
v\in\mathbb{R}
.
That is, for
\beta=1
, the limit law
\mathcal{L}\big(f_{1}^{*,\ell}(0)\big)
coincides indeed with a scaled Chernoff distribution.

\bullet

Without affecting the Chernoff-type limiting shape in the slow regime, the rate of consistency accelerates from the classical rate
n^{\beta/(2\beta+1)}
to

(n/\delta_{n})^{\beta/(2\beta+1)}

according to the level of feature impact.

\bullet

In the fast regime, rate of convergence and limiting distribution coincide with the one for flat functions in classical asymptotics as derived mutatis mutandis in Theorem 2.4 of
Jankowski (2014)
for the Grenander estimator.

\bullet

With the new intermediate regime (
n\delta_{n}^{2\beta}\longrightarrow c\in(0,\infty)
) at the phase transition, the picture is completed by the new limiting distribution, which is different from the other two occurring distributions and does not show up in classical asymptotics. It is the so-far missing approximating limiting distribution, visualized in Figure
3
.

The new limiting distribution in the intermediate regime depends continuously on
c
in the topology of weak convergence. As the next result shows for
\beta=1
, it converges to the limit in (iii) for
c\longrightarrow 0
, whereas a rescaled version converges to the limiting distribution in (i) for
c\longrightarrow\infty
.

Theorem 3.3

(Continuity)
.

Let
x_{0}
be an interior point of
\mathcal{X}
and assume
\Phi_{0}
to be continuously differentiable in a neighborhood of zero with non-vanishing derivative in zero. Then,

\displaystyle c^{-1/6}g_{1,c}^{*,\ell}(F_{X}(x_{0}))

\displaystyle\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{L}}f_{1}^{*,\ell}(0)\quad

\displaystyle\text{as }c\longrightarrow\infty,

\displaystyle g_{1,c}^{*,\ell}(F_{X}(x_{0}))

\displaystyle\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{L}}g_{1,0}^{*,\ell}(F_{X}(x_{0}))\quad

\displaystyle\text{as }c\longrightarrow 0.

The proof is given in Section
6
.

Remark
.

It is insightful to relate Theorem
3.2
to the interesting recent work of
Mallick et al. (2023)
(MSK for short). Both articles, theirs and ours, study asymptotic properties of the isotonic regression estimator under a sequence of models changing with the sample size. However, the frameworks considered in the two articles are non-nested: We describe a global property, whereas MSK focuses on a local property and correspondingly restricts their attention to pointwise limiting results. In the pointwise case, we characterize a new “intermediate” limiting distribution, which lies between the two cases well-established in the literature and which cannot be learnt from the work of MSK. On the other hand, while MSK only considers the slow regime, the class of drift terms of the Gaussian process in the limiting distribution is richer than the one considered in here, due to the fact that beyond the scaling, their triangular array allows also for varying qualitative properties of the elements of
(\Phi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
.

4
A global case

As a weak feature-label relation constitutes a global property, it is natural to study its implications on a global distance like the
L^{1}
-error. In the classical
L^{1}
-asymptotics for strictly isotonic regression functions, the already parametric
\sqrt{n}
-convergence rate in the limit theorem of the appropriately centered
L^{1}
-error raises the question whether the phase transition was a purely pointwise phenomenon. Indeed, a new effect will be shown to emerge here, namely an interplay of the rate of convergence of the so-called inverse process and the
L^{1}
-convergence rate of the NPMLE, which do not coincide in the weak-feature-impact scenario any longer.
To properly uncover this phenomenon, we first complement our pointwise lower bounds by
L^{1}
-risk local minimax lower bounds and prove that they are adaptively attained by the NPMLE in the weak-feature-impact scenario (Section
4.1
). On this basis, the main result in Section
4.2
is the second order asymptotic of the
L^{1}
-error, which turns out to behave fundamentally different to the pointwise case and is considerably harder to derive.

A generalization of the main result to the
L^{p}
-distance for
p>1
in the slow regime might be possible, but an essential step in the proof is to approximate the
L^{1}
-distance of
\hat{\Phi}_{n}
and
\Phi_{n}
by the integral of their inverse counterparts, where the inverse process of
\hat{\Phi}_{n}
comes into play. In case of the
L^{p}
-distance, additional terms arise here and bounding the emerging expressions becomes heavily technical. In the fast regime, on the other hand, an essential argument breaks down completely (cf. (
7.2
)), when the
L^{1}
-distance is replaced by the
L^{p}
-distance. An extension to
p>1
for the fast regime is thus far from straightforward.

4.1
L^{1}
-risk local minimax lower bounds and adaptivity of the NPMLE

Theorem 4.1

(
{L^{1}}
-risk lower bound)
.

Recalling the definition in (
3.2
) of the restricted classes from Section
3.1
, we have

\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\inf_{\delta\in[0,\frac{1}{4T}]}\inf_{T_{n}^{\delta}}\sup_{\Phi\in\mathcal{F}_{\delta}}\Big(\sqrt{n}\wedge\Big(\frac{n}{\delta}\Big)^{1/3}\Big)\mathbb{E}_{\Phi}^{\otimes n}\bigg[\int_{-T}^{T}\big|T_{n}^{\delta}(t)-\Phi(t)\big|dt\bigg]>0,

where the infimum is running over all estimators
T_{n}^{\delta}=T_{n}^{\delta}\big(\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}},(x_{1},y_{1}),\dots,(x_{n},y_{n})\big)
.

The proof, which is based on Assouad’s hypercube technique (cf. Theorem 2.12
Tsybakov (2009)
), is deferred to Section
C.1
. The construction of the hypotheses for the slow regime is visualized in the subsequent
Figure
4
.

x_{k}
x_{k}+h_{n}
x_{k+1}
0
\varphi_{k,n}
\psi_{k,n}
1/4
3/4

Figure 4:
Left: Visualization of
\varphi_{n,k}
and
\psi_{n,k}
, which are the base functions to construct
the hypotheses. They are defined to have either slope equal to
\delta
on
(x_{k},x_{k}+h_{n})
and slope equal to

\delta/2
on
(x_{k}+h_{n},x_{k+1})
or the other way around for a partition of
[-T,T]
with step width
2h_{n}
.
Note that the pointwise
distance between these two functions at
x_{k}+h_{n}
is of order
(n/\delta)^{-1/3}
, with

h_{n}\sim(n\delta^{2})^{-1/3}
.
Right: For
m\sim(n\delta^{2})^{1/3}
, the hypotheses are obtained by choosing at each of the
m
black bullets
either the blue path (i.e.
\psi_{n,k}
) or the orange path
(i.e.
\varphi_{n,k}
), resulting in
2^{m}
graphs corresponding to different hypotheses
functions.

In preparation for the limiting distribution theory, the next proposition shows that this faster rate of convergence for the
L^{1}
-risk is actually adaptively attained by the NPMLE in the weak-feature-impact scenario. In particular, the transition from the nonparametric to the parametric regime shows up again at the level of feature impact
\delta=\delta_{n}\sim n^{-1/2}
.

Proposition 4.2
.

Suppose that
\Phi_{0}
is continuously differentiable with
\Phi_{0}^{\prime}(0)>0
. Then,

\Big(\sqrt{n}\wedge\Big(\frac{n}{\delta_{n}}\Big)^{1/3}\Big)\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_{-T}^{T}\big|\hat{\Phi}_{n}(t)-\Phi_{n}(t)\big|dt\bigg]=\mathcal{O}(1)

in the weak-feature-impact scenario.

Although local adaptivity properties of the NPMLE for the global estimation problem were derived in
Chatterjee et al. (2015)
and
Bellec (2018)
, Proposition
4.2
is not covered by those results. Note in particular that the sharp oracle inequality of
Bellec (2018)
in Euclidean norm for monotone vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
has an additional logarithmic factor in the parametric regime that we do not observe here.

Preview of the proof.
Arguing in the slow regime (
n\delta_{n}^{2}\longrightarrow\infty
) along the spirit of
Durot (2007)
and
Durot (2008)
(to actually get the bound in expectation rather than in probability), the proof is quite elucidating. On basis of Fubini’s theorem and partial integration, the idea is to rewrite

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\int_{-T}^{T}

\displaystyle\big|\hat{\Phi}_{n}(t)-\Phi_{n}(t)\big|dt=\int_{-T}^{T}\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{P}\big(\hat{\Phi}_{n}(t)-\Phi_{n}(t)>x\big)dx\,dt+\int_{-T}^{T}\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{P}\big(\Phi_{n}(t)-\hat{\Phi}_{n}(t)>x\big)dx\,dt

to employ the switch relation (Lemma
2.2
) in the probabilities inside the integrals, giving

\mathbb{P}\big(\hat{\Phi}_{n}(t)-\Phi_{n}(t)>x\big)=\mathbb{P}\big(F_{n}^{-1}\circ\tilde{U}_{n}(\Phi_{n}(t)+x)<F_{n}^{-1}(F_{n}(t))\big)

(exemplarily for the left-hand side), and to derive by means of the slicing device and the
Dvoretzky et al. (1956)
inequality
a tail bound (Lemma
4.4
) for the process
F_{n}^{-1}\circ\tilde{U}_{n}-\Phi_{n}^{-1}
.
This is the moment where the level of feature impact
\delta_{n}
, i.e. the exact dependence on the derivative
\Phi_{n}^{\prime}
, starts to matter. Its occurence has to be traced back for being incorporated explicitly
but notably in the tail inequality. Whereas NPMLE and inverse process both scale at the rate
n^{1/3}
in the classical asymptotics, their convergence rates do not coincide in the weak-feature-impact scenario any longer: As the tail inequality in Lemma
4.4
reveals, the inverse process scales pointwise at the rate
(n\delta_{n}^{2})^{1/3}
. It is insightful to contrast its rate with
the convergence rate
(n/\delta_{n})^{1/3}
of the NPMLE.
In the parametric regime (
n\delta_{n}^{2}=\mathcal{O}(1)
), arguing by means of the inverse process is
subtle as it is not everywhere convergent any longer. However, the interval of non-convergence turns out to have length of order
\delta_{n}
only.
Combining this with sufficiently fast convergence outside this interval bounds the expected
L^{1}
-error in the fast regime.
The complete proof is given in Section
C.2
.

4.2
Distributional transition of the
L^{1}
-error

Our final aim is to study the second order asymptotics of the stabilized
L^{1}
-error (cf. Proposition
4.2
)

\Big(\sqrt{n}\wedge\Big(\frac{n}{\delta_{n}}\Big)^{1/3}\Big)\int_{-T}^{T}|\hat{\Phi}_{n}(t)-\Phi_{n}(t)|dt,

i.e. to investigate the stochastic fluctuation around an appropriate centering
\mu_{n}=\mathcal{O}(1)
.
For this, let us define for ease of notation
X(a)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\operatorname*{argmin}_{s\in\mathbb{R}}\{Z(s)+(s-a)^{2}\}
for
a\in\mathbb{R}
, as well as

\mu_{n}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbb{E}[|X(0)|]\int_{-T}^{T}\bigg(\frac{4\Phi_{n}(t)(1-\Phi_{n}(t))\Phi_{0}^{\prime}(\delta_{n}t)}{p_{X}(t)}\bigg)^{1/3}dt.

Note that
\mathcal{L}(X(0))
is the Chernoff distribution and that, indeed,
\mu_{n}=\mathcal{O}(1)
.
Next, set

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\operatorname{Cov}(|X(0)|,|X(a)-a|)da\quad\text{and}\quad\sigma^{2}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=8\,\mathcal{C}\int_{-T}^{T}\frac{\Phi_{0}(0)(1-\Phi_{0}(0))}{p_{X}(t)}dt.

(4.1)

The next theorem shows that as in the pointwise asymptotics studied in Section
3
, the second order asymptotic of the NPMLE’s stabilized
L^{1}
-error exhibits a phase transition. In contrast to the pointwise asymptotics, however, the rate of convergence diminishes in the slow regime and collapses at the phase transition. Indeed, it uncovers an interplay of the newly derived convergence rates
(n/\delta_{n})^{1/3}
of the NPMLE and
(n\delta_{n}^{2})^{1/3}
of the inverse process, which cannot be read off in the classical asymptotics. To the best of our knowledge, the limiting distribution in the fast regime is likewise new and has not even been discovered in classical asymptotics for flat functions. Our proof in here develops a new strategy, not based on the switch relation.

Theorem 4.3
.

Let
\Phi_{0}
be differentiable in a neighborhood of zero with

\Phi_{0}^{\prime}(0)>0
.

(i)

(Slow regime) Let
p_{X}
be continuously differentiable on
[-T,T]
(one-sided at
-T,T
) and assume that
\Phi_{0}^{\prime}
is Hölder-continuous in a neighborhood of zero. If
n\delta_{n}^{2}\longrightarrow\infty
, then

(n\delta_{n}^{2})^{1/6}\bigg(\Big(\frac{n}{\delta_{n}}\Big)^{1/3}\int_{-T}^{T}|\hat{\Phi}_{n}(t)-\Phi_{n}(t)|dt-\mu_{n}\bigg)\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{L}}N\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2})\quad\text{as }n\longrightarrow\infty.

(ii)

(Fast regime)
Let
\Phi_{0}^{\prime}
be continuous in a neighborhood of zero. If
n\delta_{n}^{2}\longrightarrow 0
, then

\sqrt{n}\int_{-T}^{T}|\hat{\Phi}_{n}(x)-\Phi_{n}(x)|dP_{X}(x)\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{L}}\max_{s\in[-T,T]}A(s)\quad\text{as }n\longrightarrow\infty,

where
(A(s))_{s\in[-T,T]}
is a
continuous, centered Gaussian process with
A(-T)=-A(T)
and

\operatorname{Cov}(A(s),A(t))=\Phi_{0}(0)(1-\Phi_{0}(0))(1-2|F_{X}(s)-F_{X}(t)|)\quad\text{for }s,t\in[-T,T].

Note that statement (ii) can be turned into the convergence of

\frac{\sqrt{n}}{2T}\int_{-T}^{T}|\hat{\Phi}_{n}(t)-\Phi_{n}(t)|dt

in case the feature is uniformly distributed. The proof of the theorem extends over Section
7
and Section
C.3
, employing auxiliary results as visualized in Figure
5
.

4.3
7
4.2
C.2
C.1
4.4
4.3
C.3
2.4

Figure 5:
Structural interrelation of the proofs of Section
4
and their auxiliary results.

\bullet

Without affecting the limiting normal distribution and in contrast to the accelerating rate according to Proposition
4.2
, the rate of the stabilized
L^{1}
-error slows down to
(n\delta_{n}^{2})^{1/6}
and collapses at the phase transition
\delta_{n}\sim n^{-1/2}
.

\bullet

As already mentioned in Section
4.1
,
(n\delta_{n}^{2})^{1/3}
is the convergence rate of the inverse process, which will be shown to actually drive the convergence in (i), and this inverse process is not convergent any longer if
n\delta_{n}^{2}=\mathcal{O}(1)
.

\bullet

To derive the new limit in the fast regime (ii), arguing by means of the inverse process is therefore not reasonable any longer. Instead, we utilize Corollary
2.4
to move over to an integral with respect to the empirical feature distribution in order to exploit the characterization (
2.2
), which in turn allows to approximate the resulting empirical
L^{1}
-error by a supremum over a centered partial sum process.

\bullet

The limiting distributions in the intermediate regime (
n\delta_{n}^{2}\longrightarrow c\in(0,\infty)
) remain an open problem.

4.3
Auxiliary results on the inverse process

The following result is a key ingredient for the proofs of
Proposition
4.2
and Theorem
4.3
(i). Recall the definition of the inverse process
\tilde{U}_{n}

in (
2.3
) and define
\lambda_{n}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\Phi_{n}\circ F_{X}^{-1}
.

Lemma 4.4
.

Suppose that
\Phi_{0}
is continuously differentiable with
\Phi_{0}^{\prime}(0)>0
. Then, for any
q\geq 2
, there exist
constants
C=C(\Phi_{0},p_{X},q)>0
and
N_{0}=N_{0}(\Phi_{0},(\delta_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}},q)\in\mathbb{N}
, such that for every

n\geq N_{0}
,
a\in[0,1]
and
x>0
,

(i)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(|\tilde{U}_{n}(a)-\lambda_{n}^{-1}(a)|\geq x\big)\leq\mathds{1}_{\{x\in[0,(n\delta_{n}^{2})^{-1/3})\}}+\frac{C}{(n\delta_{n}^{2}x^{3})^{q/2}}\mathds{1}_{\{x\in[(n\delta_{n}^{2})^{-1/3},1]\}}
,

(ii)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(|F_{n}^{-1}(\tilde{U}_{n}(a))-\Phi_{n}^{-1}(a)|\geq x\big)\leq\mathds{1}_{\{x\in[0,(n\delta_{n}^{2})^{-1/3})\}}+\frac{C}{(n\delta_{n}^{2}x^{3})^{q/2}}\mathds{1}_{\{x\in[(n\delta_{n}^{2})^{-1/3},2T]\}}
.

The proof is given in Section
E.1
. Interestingly, tight bounds on
\Phi_{n}^{\prime}
, both from above and from below, enter its derivation. Therefore, the tail bound crucially depends on the fact that the level of feature impact
\delta_{n}
actually precisely characterizes the speed with which the gradient of the feature-label relation approaches zero (uniformly on compacts).

Corollary 4.5
.

Suppose
\Phi_{0}
to be continuously differentiable with
\Phi_{0}^{\prime}(0)>0
. For
i=1,2
, let
(Z_{i,n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a
sequence of
\mathbb{R}
-valued random variables with
|Z_{i,n}|\leq c_{n}
for some sequence
(c_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
. Then, for any

q\geq 2
and any
r\in[1,3q/2)
, there exist
C=C(\Phi_{0},p_{X},q)>0
and
N_{0}=N_{0}(\Phi_{0},(\delta_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}},q)\in\mathbb{N}
, such that for every

n\geq N_{0}
,
a\in[0,1]
and
Z_{i,n}\in[-a,1-a]
,

\mathbb{E}\big[\big|\tilde{U}_{n}(a+Z_{1,n})-\lambda_{n}^{-1}(a+Z_{2,n})\big|^{r}\big]\leq C\min\Big\{(n\delta_{n}^{2})^{-r/3}+\Big(\frac{c_{n}}{\delta_{n}}\Big)^{r},1\Big\}.

The proof is deferred to Section
E.2
, utilizing monotonicity of both
\tilde{U}_{n}
and

\lambda_{n}^{-1}
. For
c_{n}=0
, we obtain an upper bound on the pointwise risk of the inverse process.

5
Further statistical implications and open problems

Apart from explaining the small-sample effect on the NPMLE and identifying
n\delta_{n}^{2}
(corresponding to
(\sqrt{n}\cdot\Phi_{n}^{\prime})^{2}
) as the key quantity which governs the full picture of limiting distributions in case of a non-vanishing derivative, our results reveal further statistical consequences sketched below.

A new hypotheses test and its optimal power against local alternatives.
Based on
n
i.i.d. observations
(X_{1},Y_{1}),\dots,(X_{n},Y_{n})
in the isotonic binary regression model with uniformly distributed
X_{i}\sim\mathcal{U}([-T,T])
, we consider the testing problem

H_{0}\ :\ \Phi_{|[-T,T]}=c_{0}\qquad\text{versus}\qquad H_{1}\ :\ \Phi\in\mathcal{F}\ \text{with}\ \Phi(0)=c_{0}\ \text{and}\ \inf_{\nu}\frac{\omega_{\nu}^{[-T,T]}(\Phi)}{\nu}>0,

with
c_{0}\in(0,1)
and where
\omega_{\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}}^{[-T,T]}
is defined in (
3.1
). We propose the test statistic

S_{n}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{\sqrt{n}}{2T}\int_{-T}^{T}|\hat{\Phi}_{n}(x)-c_{0}|dx

and the corresponding test

T_{n}\big((X_{1},Y_{1}),\dots,(X_{n},Y_{n})\big)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{cases}0,&\quad\text{if }S_{n}\leq\kappa_{\alpha},\\
1,&\quad\text{else},\end{cases}

with
\kappa_{\alpha}
the
(1-\alpha)
-quantile of the limiting distribution derived in Theorem
4.3
(ii) with
\Phi_{0}(0)=c_{0}
. Note that the limiting distribution of the
L^{1}
-error and in particular the quantiles under the simple null were previously unknown. Hence, the test itself could not have been developed before. As a consequence of Theorem
4.3
(i), this test is consistent against local alternatives
\Phi_{n}(x)=\Phi_{0}(\delta_{n}x)
with
\Phi_{0}\in H_{1}
in the whole slow regime. Indeed, if
n\delta_{n}^{2}\longrightarrow\infty
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\Phi_{n}}(T_{n}=1)\geq\

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\Phi_{n}}\bigg(\frac{\sqrt{n}}{2T}\int_{-T}^{T}|\Phi_{n}(x)-c_{0}|dx-\frac{\sqrt{n}}{2T}\int_{-T}^{T}|\hat{\Phi}_{n}(x)-\Phi_{n}(x)|dx>\kappa_{\alpha}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\Phi_{n}}\bigg(\frac{(n\delta_{n}^{2})^{1/6}}{2T}\bigg(\Big(\frac{n}{\delta_{n}}\Big)^{1/3}\int_{-T}^{T}|\Phi_{n}(x)-c_{0}|dx-\mu_{n}\bigg)

\displaystyle\qquad\quad-\frac{(n\delta_{n}^{2})^{1/6}}{2T}\bigg(\Big(\frac{n}{\delta_{n}}\Big)^{1/3}\int_{-T}^{T}|\hat{\Phi}_{n}(x)-\Phi_{n}(x)|dx-\mu_{n}\bigg)>\kappa_{\alpha}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\Phi_{n}}\bigg(\frac{(n\delta_{n}^{2})^{1/6}}{2T}\big(O((n\delta_{n}^{2})^{1/3})-\mu_{n}\big)

\displaystyle\qquad\quad-\frac{(n\delta_{n}^{2})^{1/6}}{2T}\bigg(\Big(\frac{n}{\delta_{n}}\Big)^{1/3}\int_{-T}^{T}|\hat{\Phi}_{n}(x)-\Phi_{n}(x)|dx-\mu_{n}\bigg)>\kappa_{\alpha}\bigg)\longrightarrow 1\quad\text{as }n\longrightarrow\infty,

whereas by Theorem
4.3
(ii), the test
T_{n}
has no power for
n\delta_{n}^{2}\longrightarrow 0
, as

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\Phi_{n}}(T_{n}=1)\leq\mathbb{P}_{\Phi_{n}}\bigg(\frac{\sqrt{n}}{2T}\int_{-T}^{T}|\hat{\Phi}_{n}(x)-\Phi_{n}(x)|dx+o(1)>\kappa_{\alpha}\bigg)\longrightarrow\alpha\quad\text{for }n\longrightarrow\infty.

Note that no test can achieve non-trivial power against these local alternatives
\Phi_{n}(\cdot)=\Phi_{0}(\delta_{n}\ \cdot)
in the fast regime, because, with
P_{0}
representing the distribution under the null,

\displaystyle h^{2}\Big(P_{0}^{\otimes n},P_{\Phi_{n}}^{\otimes n}\Big)=2\Bigg(1-\bigg(1-\frac{h^{2}(P_{0},P_{\Phi_{n}})}{2}\bigg)^{n}\Bigg)\longrightarrow 0\quad\text{iff }n\delta_{n}^{2}\longrightarrow 0

by elementary algebra, where
h
denotes the Hellinger metric from (
2.4
). That is, our test achieves the optimal separation rate against these types of local alternatives.

Remark
.

In the context of hypotheses testing, a more natural formulation for the local alternatives is given by
\overline{\Phi}_{n}(x)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=c_{0}+\delta_{n}f(x)
,
for some strictly isotonic function
f_{|[-T,T]}\colon[-T,T]\to[-c_{0},1-c_{0}]
. The results of Section
3
for
\beta=1
, as well as the results of Section
4
carry over mutatis mutandis for
\Phi_{n}
replaced by
\overline{\Phi}_{n}
.
Solely for
\beta>1
, the convergence rate and phase transition are affected, as in this case
\overline{\Phi}_{n}^{(\beta)}(x)=\delta_{n}f^{(\beta)}(x)
, i.e. the rate at which
\overline{\Phi}_{n}^{(\beta)}
converges to zero is now the same for all
\beta
. An appealing aspect of inserting
\delta_{n}
inside the regression function in binary regression is that
\Phi_{n}(\mathbb{R})
of the local alternatives remains unchanged, as is the case in logistic regression, and
\Phi_{n}
converges for the opposite case
\delta_{n}\longrightarrow\infty
to the step function, i.e. the label is then measurable with respect to the feature.

The new benchmark for distributional approximation.

Subsequently, we use the abbreviation
\sigma_{x_{0}}^{2}(f)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=f(x_{0})(1-f(x_{0}))
,
f\in\mathcal{F}
and note that
\sigma_{x_{0}}^{2}(\Phi_{n})
can be consistently estimated by the plug-in version
\sigma_{x_{0}}^{2}(\hat{\Phi}_{n})
when
\Phi_{0}(0)\in(0,1)
. Recalling
\Phi_{n}^{\prime}(x_{0})\asymp\delta_{n}
in case of a non-vanishing derivative
\Phi_{0}^{\prime}(0)>0
, an immediate consequence of Theorem
3.3
is that then the Chernoff approximation

\displaystyle\Bigg(\frac{n}{\sigma_{x_{0}}^{2}(\Phi_{n})\Phi_{n}^{\prime}(x_{0})}\Bigg)^{1/3}\big(\hat{\Phi}_{n}(x_{0})-\Phi_{n}(x_{0})\big)\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{L}}\bigg(\frac{4}{p_{X}(x_{0})}\bigg)^{1/3}\operatorname*{argmin}_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\big\{Z(t)+t^{2}\big\}\quad\text{for }n\longrightarrow\infty

continues to hold iff
n\!\cdot\!\Phi_{n}^{\prime}(x_{0})^{2}\longrightarrow\infty
. Under this condition, if
\widehat{\Phi_{n}^{\prime}}(x_{0})-\Phi_{n}^{\prime}(x_{0})=o_{\mathbb{P}}(\Phi_{n}^{\prime}(x_{0}))
for some derivative estimator
\widehat{\Phi_{n}^{\prime}}
of
\Phi_{n}^{\prime}
, the approximation remains valid for

\Bigg(\frac{n}{\sigma_{x_{0}}^{2}(\hat{\Phi}_{n})\widehat{\Phi_{n}^{\prime}}(x_{0})}\Bigg)^{1/3}\big(\hat{\Phi}_{n}(x_{0})-\Phi_{n}(x_{0})\big)

by Slutsky’s lemma. Of course, this required speed
\widehat{\Phi_{n}^{\prime}}(x_{0})-\Phi_{n}^{\prime}(x_{0})=o_{\mathbb{P}}(\Phi_{n}^{\prime}(x_{0}))
limits applicability in case of small derivatives
\Phi_{n}^{\prime}(x_{0})
and especially does not allow to exploit the Chernoff approximation in the full slow regime as long as no additional data on the derivative is available. Note that the popular method by
Bertail et al. (1999)
to construct confidence intervals in case of unknown rates of convergence is not applicable here a priori, as the subsampling procedure leads to a reduced sample size, thus changing the relation of sample size and steepness of the regression function.

However, as pointed out in
Hall and Yatchew (2007)
, there do exist situations where additional data on the derivatives is present. That is, if even

\widehat{\Phi_{n}^{\prime}}(x_{0})-\Phi_{n}^{\prime}(x_{0})=o_{\mathbb{P}}(n^{-1/2}),

(5.1)

for some (partly) external derivative estimator, the distributional approximation
simultaneously in all regimes as derived in Section
3
could be exploited in its full generality. Actually, as a consequence of Theorem
3.2
and Theorem
3.3
, if
\mathbb{U}_{n,\Phi_{n}^{\prime}(x_{0})}
denotes the distribution of

\bigg\{\Big(\frac{n}{\Phi_{n}^{\prime}(x_{0})}\Big)^{1/3}\wedge\sqrt{n}\bigg\}\big(\hat{\Phi}_{n}(x_{0})-\Phi_{n}(x_{0})\big),

(5.2)

then
d_{BL}\big(\mathbb{U}_{n,\Phi_{n}^{\prime}(x_{0})},\mathbb{Q}_{n}\big)\longrightarrow 0
as
n\longrightarrow\infty
. Here,
d_{BL}
denotes the dual bounded Lipschitz metric and
\mathbb{Q}_{n}
, solely parametrized in
\sigma_{x_{0}}(\Phi_{n})
and
n\Phi_{n}^{\prime}(x_{0})^{2}
, denotes the distribution of the rescaled greatest convex minorant

\Big(\big(n\Phi_{n}^{\prime}(x_{0})^{2}\big)^{-1/6}\wedge 1\Big)\bar{g}_{n\Phi_{n}^{\prime}(x_{0})^{2}}^{*,\ell}(F_{X}(x_{0}))

for

\bar{g}_{c}(s)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\sigma_{x_{0}}(\Phi_{n})Z(s)+\sqrt{c}\,\mathbb{E}\Big[(X-x_{0})\mathds{1}_{\{X\leq F_{X}^{-1}(s)\}}\Big].

Indeed, as visualized in Figure
3
, the new distribution
\mathbb{Q}_{n}
(orange line) provides a significantly better distributional approximation of
\mathbb{U}_{n,\Phi_{n}^{\prime}(x_{0})}
(blue line), as compared to both, the Chernoff distribution (black line), as well as the limiting distribution for flat functions (grey line). Hereby, the distribution
\mathbb{Q}_{n}
, which does not degenerate as
n\longrightarrow\infty
, provides a new benchmark for distributional approximation of the NPMLE.
In case of (
5.1
), we can replace the quantity
\Phi_{n}^{\prime}(x_{0})
by its estimate in the rate in (
5.2
), without affecting the limiting distribution. Likewise, as
\mathbb{Q}_{n}
depends continuously on
n\Phi_{n}^{\prime}(x_{0})^{2}
and
\sigma_{x_{0}}(\Phi_{n})
,

d_{BL}\big(\widehat{\mathbb{Q}}_{n},\mathbb{Q}_{n}\big)\longrightarrow_{\mathbb{P}}0\quad\text{as }n\longrightarrow\infty,

in view of Theorem
3.3
, where
\widehat{\mathbb{Q}}_{n}
arises from
\mathbb{Q}_{n}
by inserting the respective estimators
\sigma_{x_{0}}(\hat{\Phi}_{n})
and
n\widehat{\Phi_{n}^{\prime}}(x_{0})^{2}
, noting that (
5.1
) reveals

n\widehat{\Phi_{n}^{\prime}}(x_{0})^{2}=n\Phi_{n}^{\prime}(x_{0})^{2}+\big(1+\sqrt{n}\Phi_{n}^{\prime}(x_{0})\big)o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)
. In total,

\displaystyle d_{BL}\Bigg(\mathcal{L}\bigg(\bigg\{\bigg(\frac{n}{\widehat{\Phi_{n}^{\prime}}(x_{0})}\bigg)^{1/3}\wedge\sqrt{n}\bigg\}\big(\hat{\Phi}_{n}(x_{0})-\Phi_{n}(x_{0})\big)\bigg),\widehat{\mathbb{Q}}_{n}\Bigg)\longrightarrow_{\mathbb{P}}0\quad\text{as }n\longrightarrow\infty.

Finally, as
\Phi_{n}^{\prime}(x_{0})
is involved unpleasantly in the rate of convergence of the NPMLE as well as in the new approximating distribution
\mathbb{Q}_{n}
and (
5.1
) cannot be taken for granted in general, the convergence

d_{BL}\big(\mathbb{U}_{n,\Phi_{n}^{\prime}(x_{0})},\mathbb{Q}_{n}\big)\longrightarrow 0\quad\text{as }n\longrightarrow\infty

simultaneously in all regimes raises the desire about a valid bootstrap approximation, thus elegantly avoiding estimation of the derivative. That is, the question is to which extent it is possible to imitate
\hat{\Phi}_{n}(x_{0})-\Phi_{n}(x_{0})
by a bootstrap statistic
T_{n}^{*}
such that

\displaystyle d_{BL}\Bigg(\mathcal{L}\bigg(\bigg\{\Big(\frac{n}{\Phi_{n}^{\prime}(x_{0})}\Big)^{1/3}\wedge\sqrt{n}\bigg\}T_{n}^{*}\,\bigg\arrowvert\,(X_{1},Y_{1}^{n}),\dots,(X_{n},Y_{n}^{n})\bigg),\mathbb{U}_{n,\Phi_{n}^{\prime}(x_{0})}\Bigg)\longrightarrow_{\mathbb{P}}0\quad\text{as }n\longrightarrow\infty,

aiming at adaptive confidence intervals for
\Phi_{n}(x_{0})
. This problem is under current investigation by the authors. The bootstrap for the
L^{1}
-error is likewise open.

A multivariate extension.
In the spirit of logistic regression, a natural extension for multivariate feature variables is given by the semiparametric relation
\mathbb{P}(Y=1|X=x)=\Phi_{0}(\beta^{\top}x)
for some isotonic
[0,1]
-valued function
\Phi
and
\beta\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, the unit sphere in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Up to now, it is still an unsolved conjecture (cf.
Balabdaoui et al. (2019)
) that the component
\hat{\beta}_{n}
of the semiparametric MLE
(\hat{\Phi}_{n},\hat{\beta}_{n})
converges at the parametric rate. To the best of our knowledge, also the semiparametric efficiency theory is still open.
As a further development, the semiparametric MLE can be studied in the weak-feature-impact triangular array, where

\mathbb{P}(Y^{n}=1|X=x)=\Phi_{n}(\beta^{\top}x)=\Phi_{0}(\delta_{n}\beta^{\top}x)
with
\delta_{n}\searrow 0
. Here, especially the rate-of-convergence question for
\hat{\beta}_{n}
in dependence on the level of feature impact is open once again. In view of our results, we expect the rate and limiting distribution of
\hat{\Phi}_{n}
to be affected by the level of feature impact, but it might influence the convergence rate and limiting distribution of
\hat{\beta}_{n}
as well. Note that the index parameter
\beta
is clearly not identifiable in the extremal case where
\Phi_{0}
is constant.

6
Proof of Theorem
3.3

The statement for
c\longrightarrow 0
is immediate. For
c\longrightarrow\infty
, let
I_{c}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=(-F_{X}(x_{0})c^{1/3},(1-F_{X}(x_{0}))c^{1/3})
and define

h_{c}\colon I_{c}\to\mathbb{R},\quad h_{c}(t)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=c^{1/6}\big(g_{1,c}(F_{X}(x_{0})+c^{-1/3}t)-g_{1,c}(F_{X}(x_{0}))\big).

Note that

\displaystyle h_{c}^{*,\ell}(t)=c^{1/6}\big(g_{1,c}(F_{X}(x_{0})+c^{-1/3}t)-g_{1,c}(F_{X}(x_{0}))\big)^{*,\ell}=c^{-1/6}g_{1,c}^{*,\ell}(F_{X}(x_{0})+c^{-1/3}t),

and consequently,

h_{c}^{*,\ell}(0)=c^{-1/6}g_{1,c}^{*,\ell}(F_{X}(x_{0})).

It thus suffices to prove the desired convergence for
h_{c}^{*,\ell}(0)
for
c\longrightarrow\infty
. For ease of notation, let

m\colon[0,1]\to\mathbb{R},\quad m(s)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\int_{-\infty}^{F_{X}^{-1}(s)}(x-x_{0})p_{X}(x)dx=\mathbb{E}[(X-x_{0})\mathds{1}_{\{X\leq F_{X}^{-1}(s)\}}]

and note that

m(s)-m(F_{X}(x_{0}))=\int_{x_{0}}^{F_{X}^{-1}(s)}(x-x_{0})p_{X}(x)dx.

Since
p_{X}
is continuous at
x_{0}
and
p_{X}(x_{0})>0
, a Taylor expansion of
F_{X}^{-1}
around
F_{X}(x_{0})
gives

F_{X}^{-1}(F_{X}(x_{0})+h)=x_{0}+\frac{h}{p_{X}(x_{0})}+o(h).

Consequently,

\displaystyle m(F_{X}(x_{0})+h)-m(F_{X}(x_{0}))=\int_{x_{0}}^{F_{X}^{-1}(F_{X}(x_{0})+h)}(x-x_{0})p_{X}(x)dx=\frac{h^{2}}{2p_{X}(x_{0})}+o(h^{2}).

Therefore, for every fixed
R>0
,

\sup_{|t|\leq R}\Big|c^{2/3}\big(m(F_{X}(x_{0})+c^{-1/3}t)-m(F_{X}(x_{0}))\big)-\frac{t^{2}}{2p_{X}(x_{0})}\Big|\longrightarrow 0\quad\text{as }c\longrightarrow\infty.

Now define

\rho_{c}\colon I_{c}\to\mathbb{R},\qquad\rho_{c}(t)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=c^{2/3}\big(m(F_{X}(x_{0})+c^{-1/3}t)-m(F_{X}(x_{0}))\big),

set
\tau\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{\Phi_{0}^{\prime}(0)}{2p_{X}(x_{0})}
and consider the process
(\tilde{h}_{c}(t))_{t\in I_{c}}
, where

\tilde{h}_{c}(t)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\sigma_{\Phi_{0}}Z(t)+\Phi_{0}^{\prime}(0)\rho_{c}(t).

Then, by basic invariance properties of the law of Brownian motion,
\tilde{h}_{c}
has the same law as
h_{c}
. Further, for

H\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R},\qquad H(t)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\sigma_{\Phi_{0}}Z(t)+\tau t^{2},

we have

\tilde{h}_{c}(t)\longrightarrow H(t)\quad\text{locally uniformly on }\mathbb{R}\text{ as }c\longrightarrow\infty.

For
a\in\mathbb{R}
, Lemma
2.1
now yields

\{\tilde{h}_{c}^{*,\ell}(0)\leq a\}=\Big\{\operatorname*{argmin^{+}}_{t\in I_{c}}\{\tilde{h}_{c}(t)-at\}\geq 0\Big\}\ \text{ and }\ \{H^{*,\ell}(0)\leq a\}=\Big\{\operatorname*{argmin^{+}}_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\{H(t)-at\}\geq 0\Big\}.

(6.1)

Since
H(t)-at=\sigma_{\Phi_{0}}Z(t)+\tau t^{2}-at\longrightarrow\infty
as
|t|\longrightarrow\infty
by the law of the iterated logarithm, the minimizer of this expression is almost surely finite and together with Theorem 2 in
Pimentel (2014)
, it is also almost surely unique. By argmin-continuous mapping (Theorem 3.2.2 of
van der Vaart and Wellner (2023)
), the local uniform convergence
\tilde{h}_{c}\longrightarrow H
then implies

\operatorname*{argmin^{+}}_{t\in I_{c}}\{\tilde{h}_{c}(t)-at\}\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{L}}\operatorname*{argmin^{+}}_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\{H(t)-at\}\quad\text{as }c\longrightarrow\infty.

Hence, by an application of an obvious adjustment of Lemma A.2 of
Cattaneo et al. (2024)
to processes defined on a compact interval (see
Cattaneo et al. (2025)
as well) and (
6.1
),
\tilde{h}_{c}^{*,\ell}(0)\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{L}}H^{*,\ell}(0)
for
c\longrightarrow\infty
and it remains to identify the law of
H^{*,\ell}(0)
. By Lemma
G.4
,

\operatorname*{argmin}_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\big\{\sigma_{\Phi_{0}}Z(t)+\tau t^{2}-at\big\}=_{\mathcal{L}}\Big(\frac{\sigma_{\Phi_{0}}}{\tau}\Big)^{2/3}\operatorname*{argmin}_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\big\{Z(t)+t^{2}\big\}+\frac{a}{2\tau}

and so together with Lemma
2.1
,

\mathbb{P}\big(H^{*,\ell}(0)\leq a\big)=\mathbb{P}\bigg(\operatorname*{argmin}_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\big\{Z(t)+t^{2}\big\}\geq-\frac{a}{2(\sigma_{\Phi_{0}}^{2}\tau)^{1/3}}\bigg)=\mathbb{P}\Big(2(\sigma_{\Phi_{0}}^{2}\tau)^{1/3}\operatorname*{argmin}_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\big\{Z(t)+t^{2}\big\}\leq a\Big).

Thus,

H^{*,\ell}(0)=_{\mathcal{L}}2(\sigma_{\Phi_{0}}^{2}\tau)^{1/3}\operatorname*{argmin}_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\big\{Z(t)+t^{2}\big\}=\bigg(\frac{4\sigma_{\Phi_{0}}^{2}\Phi_{0}^{\prime}(0)}{p_{X}(x_{0})}\bigg)^{1/3}\operatorname*{argmin}_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\big\{Z(t)+t^{2}\big\}

and the assertion follows. ∎

7
Proof of Theorem
4.3
(ii)

With
P_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{X_{i}}
denoting the empirical measure of
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
, we shall first prove
that

\sqrt{n}\int_{-T}^{T}|\hat{\Phi}_{n}(x)-\Phi_{n}(x)|dP_{X}(x)=\sqrt{n}\int_{-T}^{T}|\hat{\Phi}_{n}(x)-\Phi_{n}(x)|dP_{n}(x)+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1).

(7.1)

To this aim, we decompose

\displaystyle\sqrt{n}

\displaystyle\int_{-T}^{T}|\hat{\Phi}_{n}(x)-\Phi_{n}(x)|dP_{X}(x)

\displaystyle\qquad\quad=\sqrt{n}\int_{-T}^{T}|\hat{\Phi}_{n}(x)-\Phi_{n}(x)|d(P_{X}-P_{n})(x)+\sqrt{n}\int_{-T}^{T}|\hat{\Phi}_{n}(x)-\Phi_{n}(x)|dP_{n}(x)

and have to verify that the first term on the right-hand side converges to zero in probability. For this, let

\varepsilon>0
be
arbitrary. Setting
\Psi_{n}(\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}})\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=|\hat{\Phi}_{n}(\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}})-\Phi_{n}(\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}})|
,

I_{\eta}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=[-T+\eta,T-\eta]
and writing
\|\vbox{\hbox{\tiny$\bullet$}}\|_{I_{\eta}}
for the
\sup
-norm on
I_{\eta}
,
we have for any
\eta\in(0,T)
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigg(\sqrt{n}\bigg|\int_{-T}^{T}|\hat{\Phi}_{n}(x)-\Phi_{n}(x)|d(P_{X}-P_{n})(x)\bigg|>\varepsilon\bigg)

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\leq\mathbb{P}\bigg(\sqrt{n}\bigg|\int_{I_{\eta}}\Psi_{n}(x)d(P_{X}-P_{n})(x)\bigg|>\varepsilon/2,\|\Psi_{n}\|_{I_{\eta}}\leq\eta\bigg)+\mathbb{P}\big(\|\Psi_{n}\|_{I_{\eta}}>\eta\big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad+\mathbb{P}\bigg(\sqrt{n}\bigg|\int_{[-T,T]\setminus I_{\eta}}\Psi_{n}(x)d(P_{X}-P_{n})(x)\bigg|>\varepsilon/2\bigg).

By Corollary
2.4
,
\mathbb{P}\big(\|\Psi_{n}\|_{I_{\eta}}>\eta\big)\longrightarrow 0
as

n\longrightarrow\infty
. From Markov’s inequality, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigg(\sqrt{n}\bigg|\int_{I_{\eta}}\Psi_{n}(x)d(P_{X}-P_{n})(x)\bigg|>\varepsilon/2,\|\Psi_{n}\|_{I_{\eta}}\leq\eta\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\bigg(\sup_{g\in\mathcal{G}_{n,\eta}}\bigg|\sqrt{n}\int_{I_{\eta}}g(x)d(P_{n}-P_{X})(x)\bigg|>\varepsilon/2\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{\varepsilon}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\sup_{g\in\mathcal{G}_{n,\eta}}\bigg|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g(X_{i})-\mathbb{E}[g(X_{i})]\bigg|\bigg]

for the class

\mathcal{G}_{n,\eta}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\big\{g\colon I_{\eta}\to[0,1]\mid g=|f-\Phi_{n}|\text{ for }f\in\mathcal{F},\ \|g\|_{I_{\eta}}\leq\eta\big\}
.
Note that any
g\in\mathcal{G}_{n,\eta}
satisfies
\mathbb{E}[g(X)^{2}]\leq\eta^{2}
and
\|g\|_{I_{\eta}}\leq\eta
.
Theorem 2.14.17’ of
van der Vaart and Wellner (2023)
then reveals for some universal constant
C>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg[\sup_{g\in\mathcal{G}_{n,\eta}}\bigg|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g(X_{i})-\mathbb{E}[g(X_{i})]\bigg|\bigg]\leq CJ_{[]}\big(\eta,\mathcal{G}_{n,\eta},L^{2}(P_{X})\big)\bigg(1+\frac{J_{[]}\big(\eta,\mathcal{G}_{n,\eta},L^{2}(P_{X})\big)}{\eta^{2}\sqrt{n}}\eta\bigg).

where
J_{[]}\big(\eta,\mathcal{G}_{n,\eta},L^{2}(P_{X})\big)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\int_{0}^{\eta}\sqrt{1+\log(N_{[]}(\nu,\mathcal{G}_{n,\eta},L^{2}(P_{X}))}d\nu
denotes the bracketing integral with
\nu
-bracketing number

N_{[]}(\nu,\mathcal{G}_{n,\eta},L^{2}(P_{X}))
of
\mathcal{G}_{n,\eta}
in
L^{2}(P_{X})
.
It remains to specify a bound for the entropy with bracketing. We prove in Lemma
G.6

that

\log\big(N_{[]}\big(\nu,\mathcal{G}_{n,\eta},L^{2}(P_{X})\big)\big)\leq K\frac{(\eta+\delta_{n})}{\nu}\quad\forall\,\nu\in[0,\eta]

for some constant
K>0
independent of
n
,
\eta
and
\nu
, whence
J_{[]}\big(\eta,\mathcal{G}_{n,\eta},L^{2}(P_{X})\big)
is
bounded by
K\sqrt{(\eta+\delta_{n})\eta}
and therefore,

\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\,\mathbb{E}\bigg[\sup_{g\in\mathcal{G}_{n,\eta}}\bigg|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g(X_{i})-\mathbb{E}[g(X_{i})]\bigg|\bigg]=\mathcal{O}\big(\eta\big)\quad\text{as }\eta\longrightarrow 0.

Now note that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigg(\sqrt{n}\bigg|\int_{[-T,T]\setminus I_{\eta}}\Psi_{n}(x)d(P_{X}-P_{n})(x)\bigg|>\varepsilon/2\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\bigg(\sqrt{n}\bigg|\int_{-T}^{-T+\eta}\Psi_{n}(x)d(P_{X}-P_{n})(x)\bigg|>\varepsilon/4\bigg)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{P}\bigg(\sqrt{n}\bigg|\int_{T-\eta}^{T}\Psi_{n}(x)d(P_{X}-P_{n})(x)\bigg|>\varepsilon/4\bigg).

Similar as before, but now for the class
\mathcal{G}_{n,\eta}^{\prime}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\big\{g\colon[-T,-T+\eta]\to[0,1]\mid g=|f-\Phi_{n}|\text{ for }f\in\mathcal{F}\big\}
,

\mathbb{P}\bigg(\sqrt{n}\bigg|\int_{-T}^{-T+\eta}\Psi_{n}(x)d(P_{X}-P_{n})(x)\bigg|>\varepsilon/4\bigg)\leq\frac{4}{\varepsilon}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\sup_{g\in\mathcal{G}_{n,\eta}^{\prime}}\bigg|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g(X_{i})-\mathbb{E}[g(X_{i})]\bigg|\bigg].

Note that any
g\in\mathcal{G}_{n,\eta}^{\prime}
satisfies
\mathbb{E}[g(X)^{2}]\leq\eta\|p_{X}\|_{\infty}
and
\|g\|_{[-T,-T+\eta]}\leq 1
.
Theorem 2.14.17’ of
van der Vaart and Wellner (2023)
then reveals for some universal constant
C>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg[\sup_{g\in\mathcal{G}_{n,\eta}^{\prime}}\bigg|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g(X_{i})-\mathbb{E}[g(X_{i})]\bigg|\bigg]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\leq CJ_{[]}\big(\sqrt{\eta\|p_{X}\|_{\infty}},\mathcal{G}_{n,\eta}^{\prime},L^{2}(P_{X})\big)\bigg(1+\frac{J_{[]}\big(\sqrt{\eta\|p_{X}\|_{\infty}},\mathcal{G}_{n,\eta}^{\prime},L^{2}(P_{X})\big)}{\eta\|p_{X}\|_{\infty}\sqrt{n}}\bigg).

Again, from Lemma
G.6
,

\log\big(N_{[]}\big(\nu,\mathcal{G}_{n,\eta}^{\prime},L^{2}(P_{X})\big)\big)\leq K\frac{(1+\delta_{n})}{\nu}\quad\forall\,\nu\in\big[0,\sqrt{\eta\|p_{X}\|_{\infty}}\big]

for some constant
K>0
independent of
n
,
\eta
and
\nu
and so

J_{[]}\big(\sqrt{\eta\|p_{X}\|_{\infty}},\mathcal{F}_{\eta},L^{2}(P_{X})\big)
is bounded by

K\sqrt{1+\delta_{n}}\eta^{1/4}
. Therefore,

\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\,\mathbb{E}\bigg[\sup_{g\in\mathcal{G}_{n,\eta}^{\prime}}\bigg|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g(X_{i})-\mathbb{E}[g(X_{i})]\bigg|\bigg]=\mathcal{O}\big(\eta^{1/4}\big)\quad\text{as }\eta\longrightarrow 0.

Identical arguments hold for
\mathbb{P}\big(\sqrt{n}|\int_{T-\eta}^{T}\Psi_{n}(x)d(P_{X}-P_{n})(x)|>\varepsilon/4\big)
and so (
7.1
) is verified.

Next, we shall prove that we may replace
\Phi_{n}
by the constant
\Phi_{0}(0)
in the
L^{1}
-distance within an error of
negligible order. Here, the requirement
n\delta_{n}^{2}\longrightarrow 0
is getting essential. By the reverse triangle
inequality, a Taylor expansion of
\Phi_{n}
around
0
reveals

\displaystyle\sqrt{n}\bigg|\int_{-T}^{T}|\hat{\Phi}_{n}(x)-\Phi_{n}(x)|dP_{n}(x)-\int_{-T}^{T}|\hat{\Phi}_{n}(x)-\Phi_{0}(0)|dP_{n}(x)\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{n}\int_{-T}^{T}|\Phi_{n}(x)-\Phi_{0}(0)|dP_{n}(x)

\displaystyle=\delta_{n}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Phi_{0}^{\prime}(\delta_{n}\xi_{i}^{n})|X_{i}|

for suitable
\xi_{i}^{n}
between
0
and
X_{i}
.
Markov’s inequality combined with the assumption that
n\delta_{n}^{2}\longrightarrow 0
then yields

\sqrt{n}\int_{-T}^{T}|\hat{\Phi}_{n}(x)-\Phi_{n}(x)|dP_{n}(x)=\sqrt{n}\int_{-T}^{T}|\hat{\Phi}_{n}(x)-\Phi_{0}(0)|dP_{n}(x)+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)

and with (
7.1
), we get

\sqrt{n}\int_{-T}^{T}|\hat{\Phi}_{n}(x)-\Phi_{n}(x)|dP_{X}(x)=\sqrt{n}\int_{-T}^{T}|\hat{\Phi}_{n}(x)-\Phi_{0}(0)|dP_{n}(x)+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)
. Now, as the NPMLE is an increasing function and by Lemma
G.3
, as illustrated in
Figure
6
,

\displaystyle\int_{-T}^{T}|\hat{\Phi}_{n}(x)-\Phi_{0}(0)|dP_{n}(x)

\displaystyle=\hskip-5.39993pt\sup_{s\in[-T,T]}\hskip-1.99997pt\bigg\{\int_{s}^{T}\big(\hat{\Phi}_{n}(x)-\Phi_{0}(0)\big)dP_{n}(x)-\hskip-1.99997pt\int_{-T}^{s}\big(\hat{\Phi}_{n}(x)-\Phi_{0}(0)\big)dP_{n}(x)\bigg\}

\displaystyle=\hskip-5.39993pt\sup_{s\in[-T,T]}\hskip-1.99997pt\bigg\{\int_{-T}^{T}\big(\hat{\Phi}_{n}(x)-\Phi_{0}(0)\big)\big(1-2\mathds{1}_{\{x\leq s\}}\big)dP_{n}(x)\bigg\}.

(7.2)

-T
T
s
0
1
\Phi_{0}(0)
\hat{\Phi}_{n}
-T
T
s
0
1
\Phi_{0}(0)
\hat{\Phi}_{n}

Figure 6:
The colored area represents

\int_{s}^{T}(\hat{\Phi}_{n}(x)-\Phi_{0}(0))dP_{n}(x)-\int_{-T}^{s}(\hat{\Phi}_{n}(x)-\Phi_{0}(0))dP_{n}(x)
,
where the blue color signals a positive area w.r.t
P_{n}
and the orange color signals a negative area w.r.t
P_{n}
.
As we see, the area is maximized in the situation visualized on the right side and is equal to

\int_{-T}^{T}|\hat{\Phi}_{n}(x)-\Phi_{0}(0)|dP_{n}(x)
.

Let
T_{1}^{n},\dots,T_{j_{n}}^{n}
denote the jumping points of
\hat{\Phi}_{n}
(which are random, both in number and location) and set
T_{0}^{n}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=X_{(1)}
,
T_{j_{n}+1}^{n}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=X_{(n)}
and

T_{j_{n}+2}^{n}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=T
. Then, (
7.2
) can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\sup_{s\in[-T,T]}

\displaystyle\bigg\{\int_{-T}^{T}\big(\hat{\Phi}_{n}(x)-\Phi_{0}(0)\big)\big(1-2\mathds{1}_{\{x\leq s\}}\big)dP_{n}(x)\bigg\}

\displaystyle=\sup_{s\in[-T,T]}\Bigg\{\sum_{j=0}^{j_{n}+1}\big(\hat{\Phi}_{n}(T_{j+1}^{n})-\Phi_{0}(0)\big)\big(F_{n}(T_{j+1}^{n})-F_{n}(T_{j}^{n})\big)\big(1-2\mathds{1}_{\{T_{j+1}^{n}\leq s\}}\big)\Bigg\}.

Exploiting the characterization of the NPMLE as local sample average between two jumping points, which can be deduced from (
2.1
) and (
2.2
) (cf.
Brunk (1958)
), i.e.

\hat{\Phi}_{n}|_{(-\infty,T_{0}^{n})}=0,\quad\hat{\Phi}_{n}|_{[T_{j_{n}+1}^{n},\infty)}=\hat{\Phi}_{n}(X_{(n)}),\quad\hat{\Phi}_{n}|_{[T_{j}^{n},T_{j+1}^{n})}=\frac{\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}Y_{\ell}^{n}\mathds{1}_{\{T_{j}^{n}\leq X_{\ell}<T_{j+1}^{n}\}}}{\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}\mathds{1}_{\{T_{j}^{n}<X_{\ell}\leq T_{j+1}^{n}\}}}

for
j=0,\dots,j_{n}
, where we also agree on
\hat{\Phi}_{n}(T_{j_{n}+2}^{n})=\hat{\Phi}_{n}(X_{(n)})
, we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{j_{n}+1}\hat{\Phi}_{n}(T_{j+1}^{n})\big(F_{n}(T_{j+1}^{n})-F_{n}(T_{j}^{n})\big)\big(1-2\mathds{1}_{\{T_{j+1}^{n}\leq s\}}\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}Y_{\ell}^{n}\sum_{j=0}^{j_{n}+1}\mathds{1}_{\{T_{j}^{n}\leq X_{\ell}<T_{j+1}^{n}\}}\big(1-2\mathds{1}_{\{T_{j+1}\leq s\}}\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}Y_{\ell}^{n}\big(1-2\mathds{1}_{\{X_{\ell}\leq s\}}\big)+\frac{2}{n}\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}Y_{\ell}^{n}\mathds{1}_{\{X_{\ell}=s\}}.

Further, we have

\sum_{j=0}^{j_{n}+1}\Phi_{0}(0)\big(F_{n}(T_{j+1}^{n})-F_{n}(T_{j}^{n})\big)\big(1-2\mathds{1}_{\{T_{j+1}^{n}\leq s\}}\big)=\Phi_{0}(0)\big(1-2F_{n}(s)\big)+\frac{\Phi_{0}(0)}{n},

as well as

\Bigg|\sup_{s\in[-T,T]}\Bigg\{\frac{2}{n}\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}Y_{\ell}^{n}\mathds{1}_{\{X_{\ell}=s\}}-\frac{\Phi_{0}(0)}{n}\Bigg\}\Bigg|=o_{\mathbb{P}}(n^{-1/2}).

Now for
A_{n}\colon[-T,T]\to\mathbb{R}
denoting the continuous, piecewise linear process that satisfies

A_{n}(X_{i})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}(Y_{\ell}^{n}-\Phi_{0}(0))\big(1-2\mathds{1}_{\{X_{\ell}\leq X_{i}\}}\big)

for
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
and noting that
A_{n}
attains its maximum at the observation points, combining the previous
results shows that
\sqrt{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\hat{\Phi}_{n}(x)-\Phi_{n}(x)|dP_{X}(x)
has the same asymptotic distribution as

\sup_{s\in[-T,T]}\Bigg\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}(Y_{\ell}^{n}-\Phi_{0}(0))\big(1-2\mathds{1}_{\{X_{\ell}\leq s\}}\big)\Bigg\}=\sup_{s\in[-T,T]}\{A_{n}(s)\}=\max_{s\in[-T,T]}\{A_{n}(s)\},

where we used continuity of
A_{n}
and the fact that the process inside the
\sup
on the left-hand side changes its value only
at the observation points. Lemma
D.1
yields
A_{n}\longrightarrow_{\mathcal{L}}A
in
the space
\mathcal{C}([-T,T])
of continuous functions on
[-T,T]
, equipped with the topology of uniform convergence.
The assertion then follows from the continuous mapping theorem. ∎

4.4

Contents

A
Proofs of Section
2

In this section, the proofs of Lemma
2.2
, Proposition
2.3
, necessary auxiliary results, as well as the proof of Corollary
2.4
are given.

A.1
Proof of Lemma
2.2

Using the notation introduced in Section
2
, we have
\hat{\Phi}_{n}(x)=g_{n}\circ F_{n}(x)
, where
g_{n}
denoted the left-derivative of
G_{n}
, which is the greatest convex minorant of
\Upsilon_{n}
. Thus, for
x\in\mathcal{X}
and
a\in[0,1]
,

\hat{\Phi}_{n}(x)>a\quad\iff\quad g_{n}\circ F_{n}(x)>a.

By Lemma
2.1
and the definition of
\tilde{U}_{n}
, we obtain

g_{n}(F_{n}(x))>a\quad\iff\quad\operatorname*{argmin^{+}}_{u\in[0,1]}\{\Upsilon_{n}(u)-au\}<F_{n}(x)\quad\iff\quad\tilde{U}_{n}(a)<F_{n}(x).

As
F_{n}^{-1}
is monotonically increasing and both
F_{n}
and
\tilde{U}_{n}
map into the set
\{i/n\mid i=0,\dots,n\}
, this is equivalent to

F_{n}^{-1}\circ\tilde{U}_{n}(a)<F_{n}^{-1}\circ F_{n}(x)

and the assertion follows from
F_{n}^{-1}\circ\tilde{U}_{n}(a)=U_{n}(a)
. ∎

A.2
Proof of Proposition
2.3

Before we start with the actual proof,
let us introduce for every
\Psi\in\mathcal{F}
the functions

\displaystyle f_{\Psi,\Phi}\colon\mathbb{R}\times\{0,1\}\to\bar{\mathbb{R}},

\displaystyle\quad f_{\Psi,\Phi}(x,y)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{p_{\Psi}(x,y)+p_{\Phi}(x,y)}{2p_{\Phi}(x,y)},

\displaystyle m_{\Psi,\Phi}\colon\mathbb{R}\times\{0,1\}\to\bar{\mathbb{R}},

\displaystyle\quad m_{\Psi,\Phi}(x,y)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\log(f_{\Psi,\Phi}(x,y)),

where
\bar{\mathbb{R}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty,\infty\}
and we agree on
\frac{c}{0}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\infty
for
c\geq 0
. Further, we define for every
n\in\mathbb{N}

M_{n}(\Psi,\Phi)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}m_{\Psi,\Phi}(x_{i},y_{i})

and their expectation under
P_{\Phi}^{\otimes n}
,

M(\Psi,\Phi)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathbb{E}_{\Phi}[m_{\Psi,\Phi}(X,Y)].

Note that for the set
A_{\Phi}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\{p_{\Phi}>0\}
, we have
P_{\Phi}(A_{\Phi})=1
, so
f_{\Psi,\Phi}
and
m_{\Psi,\Phi}
are finite outside of a set of measure zero and we can work on the intersection with
A_{\Phi}
for the remainder of this proof whenever necessary. Note further that
\Phi
is identifiable by definition and that
M_{n}(\Phi,\Phi)=M(\Phi,\Phi)=0
by definition of

m_{\Psi,\Phi}
. The following Lemma guarantees
M_{n}(\hat{\Phi}_{n},\Phi)\geq M_{n}(\Phi,\Phi)=0
for every

n\in\mathbb{N}
, which
is a weaker statement than
\hat{\Phi}_{n}
nearly maximizing
M_{n}
, but still suffices for the consistency proof.

Lemma A.1
.

For every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we have
M_{n}(\hat{\Phi}_{n},\Phi)\geq 0

P_{\Phi}^{\otimes n}
-almost surely.

By concavity of the logarithm and the definition of
\hat{\Phi}_{n}
as the maximizer of the log-likelihood, we have
P_{\Phi}^{\otimes n}
-almost surely

\displaystyle M_{n}(\hat{\Phi}_{n},\Phi)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log\bigg(\frac{p_{\hat{\Phi}_{n}}(x_{i},y_{i})+p_{\Phi}(x_{i},y_{i})}{2p_{\Phi}(x_{i},y_{i})}\bigg)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{2}\log\bigg(\frac{p_{\hat{\Phi}_{n}}(x_{i},y_{i})}{p_{\Phi}(x_{i},y_{i})}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log(p_{\hat{\Phi}_{n}}(x_{i},y_{i}))-\log(p_{\Phi}(x_{i},y_{i}))

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log(p_{\Phi}(x_{i},y_{i}))-\log(p_{\Phi}(x_{i},y_{i}))=0.

∎

The following Lemma guarantees that
\Phi
is a well-separated point of maximum of
M(\cdot,\Phi)
.

Lemma A.2
.

For every
\Psi,\Phi\in\mathcal{F}
, we have
